## IT-C001: Recorded annual counts rose from 2021 to 2025

Status: Candidate

**Proposed wording**

In the September 27, 2026 export, the number of incident records dated in each year increased from 80 in 2021 to 108 in 2022, 179 in 2023, 304 in 2024, and 452 in 2025. These record counts can reflect changes in reporting and coverage as well as underlying incidents.

**Calculation confidence**

High for the retained record counts and date parsing. Estimating new submissions, distinct events, or affected people would require additional data.

**Interpretation**

Low for actual incident incidence or safety changes. Moderate as a description of this database snapshot.

**Limitations**

- The unit is an incident record. Counts of distinct events, reports, or people require separate verification.
- The choice of 2021 matters: 2020 has 92 records, above 2021. Changes in reporting and coverage can change the series.
- Measuring historical backfill requires earlier exports or entry timestamps. The documented ID assignment rule alone cannot establish entry dates or past classifications.
- Voluntary reporting and changing public attention shape this collection. It lacks a representative sample and a measure of population exposure, so it cannot establish prevalence.
- Incident dates can come from legal events, reports, or publications. The export omits report dates, preventing estimates of reporting delays. ID order permits a conditional comparison, but establishing entry dates and historical backfill requires earlier exports or entry timestamps.
- Tags can change with the model or prompt. Model agreement and self-reported confidence leave classification accuracy unresolved. Nearly every record has 'High' confidence, so that field provides little help in distinguishing reliable classifications.
- The export omits source report URLs, dates, and IDs. The calculations have been checked against the export. Verifying the underlying allegations requires the source reports.
- The preceding year is included as a robustness check; the five-year pattern must not be called a trend extending indefinitely backwards.
- ID-order comparison, with database-entry timing unavailable: 2021: 60/80 (75.0%) have an ID above the minimum ID associated with the following incident year; 2022: 32/108 (29.63%) have an ID above the minimum ID associated with the following incident year; 2023: 58/179 (32.4%) have an ID above the minimum ID associated with the following incident year; 2024: 85/304 (27.96%) have an ID above the minimum ID associated with the following incident year; 2025: 103/452 (22.79%) have an ID above the minimum ID associated with the following incident year.
- Recent-month record counts are: 2026 monthly counts to date 01=38, 02=30, 03=27, 04=25, 05=35, 06=22, 07=33, 08=12, 09=9; the current year is shown only as a labeled year-to-date row.
- Records aggregating several occurrences (number_of_occurrences > 1) in 2021–2025: 2021: 14, 2022: 15, 2023: 30, 2024: 46, 2025: 66; annual record counts are not event counts.

**Affected tracker pages**

- [Tracker home](https://airisk.mit.edu/ai-incident-tracker) → How are numbers of reported AI Incidents changing over time?
- [Incident Timeline](https://airisk.mit.edu/ai-incident-tracker/incident-timeline) → Incident Timeline

## IT-C002: Capability and robustness: a falling share, a rising count

Status: Candidate

**Proposed wording**

In the September 27, 2026 export, records primarily tagged “Lack of capability or robustness” fell from 41.2% of records dated 2021 to 10.0% of those dated 2025, while their count rose from 33 to 45. Other categories grew faster. The change describes database composition and does not establish that AI systems became more robust.

**Calculation confidence**

High for primary/any-label counting and exact annual comparisons in the export.

**Interpretation**

Moderate for a compositional database statement; low for any claim that robustness has improved or failures have become rarer.

**Limitations**

- 2021 is a local high point in the longer series. The finding is specific to 2021-2025.
- The category count rose from 33 to 45; its share fell because the rest of the database grew faster.
- The partial 2026 cohort differs from 2025. It is excluded from these complete-year comparisons.
- Voluntary reporting and changing public attention shape this collection. It lacks a representative sample and a measure of population exposure, so it cannot establish prevalence.
- Incident dates can come from legal events, reports, or publications. The export omits report dates, preventing estimates of reporting delays. ID order permits a conditional comparison, but establishing entry dates and historical backfill requires earlier exports or entry timestamps.
- Tags can change with the model or prompt. Model agreement and self-reported confidence leave classification accuracy unresolved. Nearly every record has 'High' confidence, so that field provides little help in distinguishing reliable classifications.
- The export omits source report URLs, dates, and IDs. The calculations have been checked against the export. Verifying the underlying allegations requires the source reports.
- Additional risk tags answer a different question from the primary tag. Within-domain percentages and assignment shares use different denominators. The headline percentage uses all records.
- Growing counts in other categories can reduce this share while the capability/robustness count rises; the row excluding domain 4 checks how that choice affects the result. Other domains also grew, so this check cannot isolate each domain’s contribution.
- The 2021 starting point is a local maximum of the longer series (see the 'include prior years' context in facts.json annual.sub7.3.primary); the 2026 year-to-date row is context only and is never compared with whole years in the website text.
- ID-ordered subsets have these shares (2025: 8.4% of the lower-ID half versus 11.5% of the higher-ID half; 2026 to date: 12.1% versus 25.2%). Without database-entry timestamps or historical exports, these differences do not establish backfill or predict future revisions. The one preserved probable same-event pair (#230/#353) received different primary subdomains (7.3 vs 5.1).

**Affected tracker pages**

- [Incident Timeline](https://airisk.mit.edu/ai-incident-tracker/incident-timeline) → Incident Timeline

## IT-C003: Deepfake video leads the single-purpose tags

Status: Candidate

**Proposed wording**

In the September 27, 2026 export, “Deepfake Video Generation” is the most frequent single primary-purpose tag, with 316 of 1,698 records (18.6%). “Chatbot” follows with 181 (10.7%). The chart’s Other slice combines several less frequent purposes.

**Calculation confidence**

High for the primary-purpose ranking; alternate purposes are counted separately.

**Interpretation**

Moderate for the retained tag ranking; low for which AI applications are intrinsically riskiest.

**Limitations**

- The ranking depends on category breadth: related assistant uses are split across labels.
- The grouped Other slice combines the remaining purpose labels.
- These classifications do not establish which applications are intrinsically most risky.
- Voluntary reporting and changing public attention shape this collection. It lacks a representative sample and a measure of population exposure, so it cannot establish prevalence.
- Incident dates can come from legal events, reports, or publications. The export omits report dates, preventing estimates of reporting delays. ID order permits a conditional comparison, but establishing entry dates and historical backfill requires earlier exports or entry timestamps.
- Tags can change with the model or prompt. Model agreement and self-reported confidence leave classification accuracy unresolved. Nearly every record has 'High' confidence, so that field provides little help in distinguishing reliable classifications.
- The export omits source report URLs, dates, and IDs. The calculations have been checked against the export. Verifying the underlying allegations requires the source reports.
- Classifier-defined purpose categories are not equal-sized exposure groups. A leading incident count does not imply the highest risk per use.
- Purpose labels do not establish whether every implicated system actually used generative AI as described.
- For illustration, the analyst grouped the displayed categories as follows: an assistant family of Chatbot 181 + Writing Assistant 122 + Question Answering 26 + Code Generation 33 = 362; a synthetic-media family of Deepfake Video Generation 316 + Deepfake Image Generation 135 + Voice Generation 105 = 556. The ranking describes the taxonomy as displayed, whose granularity is asymmetric.
- The exploratory ledger screens rankings on records dated through the last complete year; this candidate ranks all dates (both windows are shown in the robustness table).

**Affected tracker pages**

- [Incident Overview](https://airisk.mit.edu/ai-incident-tracker/incident-overview) → Incident Overview — Interactive Chart

**Chart settings**

Exact coverage and exclusions are recorded in review/browser/BROWSER_CHECKS.md. The live mirror differs from the supplied CSV even when both contain 1,698 records; a compatible chart configuration does not guarantee identical live figures. C04 exposes nine grouping dimensions plus None, while C03 exposes thirteen dimensions.",

## IT-C004: Fraud, scams, and manipulation lead within malicious-actor records

Status: Candidate

**Proposed wording**

Among records dated 2016–2025 in the September 27, 2026 export, 368 of the 535 records primarily classified under “Malicious actors” (68.8%) have the primary subdomain “Fraud, scams and targeted manipulation.” These classifications describe the reported records within that domain. Selective reporting limits what they tell us about the prevalence of malicious AI use.

**Calculation confidence**

High for the explicitly primary, closed-window numerator and denominator.

**Interpretation**

Moderate for category composition; low for the prevalence or absolute frequency of real-world malicious AI use.

**Limitations**

- The denominator is the 535 records primarily classified under malicious actors, drawn from the 1,698-record export.
- The pooled window is dominated by recent years: 413 of the 535 records are dated 2024-2025.
- Voluntary incident reports cannot establish the prevalence of malicious AI use.
- Voluntary reporting and changing public attention shape this collection. It lacks a representative sample and a measure of population exposure, so it cannot establish prevalence.
- Incident dates can come from legal events, reports, or publications. The export omits report dates, preventing estimates of reporting delays. ID order permits a conditional comparison, but establishing entry dates and historical backfill requires earlier exports or entry timestamps.
- Tags can change with the model or prompt. Model agreement and self-reported confidence leave classification accuracy unresolved. Nearly every record has 'High' confidence, so that field provides little help in distinguishing reliable classifications.
- The export omits source report URLs, dates, and IDs. The calculations have been checked against the export. Verifying the underlying allegations requires the source reports.
- This percentage uses records in domain 4 as its denominator. Additional-domain membership changes the population.
- The default bar includes the current partial year; exact Year selection is needed for this candidate's window.
- Pooled, the within-domain 4.3 share was 71/122 (58.2%) for 2016–2023 and 297/413 (71.9%) for 2024–2025, which supply 413 of the 535 records; annual cells before 2022 hold few records and are not quoted as a range.
- The exploratory ledger screens concentrations on records dated through the last complete year without the 2016 floor; this candidate uses the bar chart's 2016 floor (both values appear in the robustness table).

**Affected tracker pages**

- [Incident Overview](https://airisk.mit.edu/ai-incident-tracker/incident-overview) → Incident Numbers by Risk Domain
- [Incident Overview](https://airisk.mit.edu/ai-incident-tracker/incident-overview) → Incident Overview — Interactive Chart

## IT-C005: Severity counts depend on including inferred harm

Status: Candidate

**Proposed wording**

In the September 27, 2026 export, 148 of 1,698 records (8.7%) meet a severity threshold of at least 4 using direct or indirect scores in the original ten harm dimensions. Including model-inferred scores raises the count to 286 (16.8%). Zero-coded cells do not count toward the threshold. Inferred scores require independent verification of the harm.

**Calculation confidence**

High for the observed score thresholds and channel maxima. The 2 zero-coded records (#413, #1607) and 0 blank-cell records have explicit bounds and an exclusion sensitivity; whether a zero score means 'not assessed' or 'no harm' is unresolved with the exporter.

**Interpretation**

High that channel choice changes the retained score count; low that inferred scores verify realized harm severity.

**Limitations**

- Inferred scores require independent verification of the harm. Zero-coded cells do not count toward the threshold. The exporter has not confirmed their meaning.
- This comparison uses the original ten harm dimensions. The public sources examined provide no category-specific threshold rubrics for the three additions: psychological harm, epistemic harm, and CSEA.
- Use a supporting table. The public headline includes inferred scores across thirteen dimensions, and its current direct-only label is misleading.
- Voluntary reporting and changing public attention shape this collection. It lacks a representative sample and a measure of population exposure, so it cannot establish prevalence.
- Incident dates can come from legal events, reports, or publications. The export omits report dates, preventing estimates of reporting delays. ID order permits a conditional comparison, but establishing entry dates and historical backfill requires earlier exports or entry timestamps.
- Tags can change with the model or prompt. Model agreement and self-reported confidence leave classification accuracy unresolved. Nearly every record has 'High' confidence, so that field provides little help in distinguishing reliable classifications.
- The export omits source report URLs, dates, and IDs. The calculations have been checked against the export. Verifying the underlying allegations requires the source reports.
- The published rubric defines the original ten dimensions. Psychological, epistemic and CSEA dimensions are reported separately as a thirteen-dimension sensitivity.
- The app defines direct and indirect scores using explicitly reported causal pathways. Inferred scores come from the model and require independent verification.
- Thresholds are ordinal. Level 1 includes none through minor harm; scores and counts are not summed into a burden-of-harm metric.

**Affected tracker pages**

- [Incident View](https://airisk.mit.edu/ai-incident-tracker/incident-view) → Harm Severity Radar / optional Severity Spider attachment
- [Incident Overview](https://airisk.mit.edu/ai-incident-tracker/incident-overview) → Incident Overview — Interactive Chart

## IT-D001: False or misleading information dominates the primary misinformation tags

Status: Candidate

**Proposed wording**

In the September 27, 2026 export, 128 of 141 records dated through 2025 and primarily classified as “Misinformation” (90.8%) have the primary subdomain “False or misleading information.” In the overview bar chart’s 2016–2025 window, the figure is 127 of 140 (90.7%). The percentage uses the model-assigned misinformation domain as its denominator. Selective reporting prevents an estimate of real-world prevalence.

**Calculation confidence**

High for the explicitly primary numerator and denominator.

**Interpretation**

Moderate for category composition; low for the prevalence of real-world events.

**Limitations**

- The denominator includes records whose primary domain is misinformation.
- The headline includes complete years before 2016; the bar chart starts at 2016. Match the window before publishing.
- The small taxonomy within this domain partly determines the concentration.
- Voluntary reporting and changing public attention shape this collection. It lacks a representative sample and a measure of population exposure, so it cannot establish prevalence.
- Incident dates can come from legal events, reports, or publications. The export omits report dates, preventing estimates of reporting delays. ID order permits a conditional comparison, but establishing entry dates and historical backfill requires earlier exports or entry timestamps.
- Tags can change with the model or prompt. Model agreement and self-reported confidence leave classification accuracy unresolved. Nearly every record has 'High' confidence, so that field provides little help in distinguishing reliable classifications.
- The export omits source report URLs, dates, and IDs. The calculations have been checked against the export. Verifying the underlying allegations requires the source reports.
- Selected by the data-driven discovery stage (score 0.930: effect 0.82, support 1.00, stability 1.00, chart_fit 0.90). Scores rank descriptive stability and chart usefulness; they are not significance tests.
- Some subdomains are rarely assigned. The concentration within a domain can therefore depend on how the classifier uses the taxonomy, as well as on the incidents described.

**Affected tracker pages**

- [Incident Overview](https://airisk.mit.edu/ai-incident-tracker/incident-overview) → Incident Numbers by Risk Domain

## IT-D002: The severity-threshold share fell while the count rose

Status: Candidate

**Proposed wording**

In the September 27, 2026 export, 35 of 80 records dated 2021 (43.8%) and 90 of 452 dated 2025 (19.9%) have a direct or indirect severity score of at least 3 in any of the original ten harm dimensions. The qualifying count rose from 35 to 90 even as its share fell. Model-inferred scores are excluded, and zero or missing scores are treated as not qualifying. Changes in reporting, coverage, and classification can affect this comparison. It cannot establish whether AI became safer.

**Calculation confidence**

High for the exported classifications and exact annual comparisons in this export.

**Interpretation**

Moderate as a description of database composition; low for any claim about real-world incidence, risk or safety.

**Limitations**

- The count meeting the threshold rose from 35 to 90 while its share fell.
- This depends on the original ten dimensions, direct/indirect channels and threshold 3.
- The present aggregate chart cannot faithfully reproduce this channel-specific calculation.
- Voluntary reporting and changing public attention shape this collection. It lacks a representative sample and a measure of population exposure, so it cannot establish prevalence.
- Incident dates can come from legal events, reports, or publications. The export omits report dates, preventing estimates of reporting delays. ID order permits a conditional comparison, but establishing entry dates and historical backfill requires earlier exports or entry timestamps.
- Tags can change with the model or prompt. Model agreement and self-reported confidence leave classification accuracy unresolved. Nearly every record has 'High' confidence, so that field provides little help in distinguishing reliable classifications.
- The export omits source report URLs, dates, and IDs. The calculations have been checked against the export. Verifying the underlying allegations requires the source reports.
- Selected by the data-driven discovery stage (score 0.896: effect 0.95, support 1.00, stability 1.00, chart_fit 0.40). Scores rank descriptive stability and chart usefulness; they are not significance tests.
- Shares are compositional: a category's share can fall while its count rises when other categories grow faster.
- Additional (non-primary) tags answer a different question from the primary tag; assignment shares are a third quantity.
- The partial current year provides context. Recent records may still be added or reclassified, so the trend comparison uses complete years.
- Severity scores are ordinal LLM annotations under the published ten-category rubric; level 1 spans none to minor harm, and zero or missing components leave the presence of harm unresolved.
- Inferred (model-imputed) scores are excluded from this direct/indirect definition; the thirteen-dimension and inferred-inclusive variants are listed as merged sensitivities.

**Chart settings**

```json
  "verification": "Representative controls, charts and incident details were exercised in the in-app browser and native Safari on September 27–28, 2026. Exact coverage and exclusions are recorded in review/browser/BROWSER_CHECKS.md. The live mirror differs from the supplied CSV even when both contain 1,698 records; a compatible chart configuration does not guarantee identical live figures. C04 exposes nine grouping dimensions plus None, while C03 exposes thirteen dimensions.",
  "filter_instructions": "Table definition: all records; direct or indirect score ≥3 in at least one of the original ten dimensions; lower_bound missing-score policy; inferred scores excluded."
}
```

## IT-D003: A larger share tagged as tool-type systems

Status: Candidate

**Proposed wording**

In the September 27, 2026 export, the share of incident records tagged “Tool” for behavior type rose each year from 2021 through 2025, from 19 of 80 (23.8%) to 256 of 452 (56.6%). Each denominator includes all records dated that year. The pattern describes database annotations and may reflect changes in reporting and coverage.

**Calculation confidence**

High for the exported classifications and exact annual comparisons in this export.

**Interpretation**

Moderate as a description of database composition; low for any claim about real-world incidence, risk or safety.

**Limitations**

- Tool is an LLM-assigned behavior tag whose meaning needs confirmation.
- The public chart has no matching grouping; use a labeled supporting table.
- The result describes the records in this database. Establishing a trend in AI deployment would require broader population data.
- Voluntary reporting and changing public attention shape this collection. It lacks a representative sample and a measure of population exposure, so it cannot establish prevalence.
- Incident dates can come from legal events, reports, or publications. The export omits report dates, preventing estimates of reporting delays. ID order permits a conditional comparison, but establishing entry dates and historical backfill requires earlier exports or entry timestamps.
- Tags can change with the model or prompt. Model agreement and self-reported confidence leave classification accuracy unresolved. Nearly every record has 'High' confidence, so that field provides little help in distinguishing reliable classifications.
- The export omits source report URLs, dates, and IDs. The calculations have been checked against the export. Verifying the underlying allegations requires the source reports.
- Selected by the data-driven discovery stage (score 0.895: effect 1.00, support 1.00, stability 1.00, chart_fit 0.30). Scores rank descriptive stability and chart usefulness; they are not significance tests.
- Shares are compositional: a category's share can fall while its count rises when other categories grow faster.
- Additional (non-primary) tags answer a different question from the primary tag; assignment shares are a third quantity.
- The partial current year provides context. Recent records may still be added or reclassified, so the trend comparison uses complete years.

Exact coverage and exclusions are recorded in review/browser/BROWSER_CHECKS.md. The live mirror differs from the supplied CSV even when both contain 1,698 records; a compatible chart configuration does not guarantee identical live figures. C04 exposes nine grouping dimensions plus None, while C03 exposes thirteen dimensions.",
```json
  "filter_instructions": "No population exclusions; target behaviour_type=Tool."
}
```

## IT-D004: Human-controlled tags grew as supervised and autonomous tags fell

Status: Candidate

**Proposed wording**

In the September 27, 2026 export, the “Human-controlled” tag accounts for 19 of 80 records dated 2021 (23.8%) and 349 of 452 dated 2025 (77.2%). Over the same period, “Human-supervised” falls from 52.5% to 14.2%, and “Full autonomy” from 23.8% to 8.6%. The national-security autonomy field covers all records in this comparison. These model-assigned tags do not measure oversight quality or changes in AI deployment.

**Calculation confidence**

High for the exported classifications and exact annual comparisons in this export.

**Interpretation**

Moderate as a description of database composition; low for any claim about real-world incidence, risk or safety.

**Limitations**

- The public framework defines Human-controlled as AI assisting while humans make final decisions. The tag’s accuracy in individual records has not been established.
- The national-security autonomy field is populated across this export. Its application outside national-security incidents requires care.
- Use a supporting table. The current public chart has no grouping for this field.
- Voluntary reporting and changing public attention shape this collection. It lacks a representative sample and a measure of population exposure, so it cannot establish prevalence.
- Incident dates can come from legal events, reports, or publications. The export omits report dates, preventing estimates of reporting delays. ID order permits a conditional comparison, but establishing entry dates and historical backfill requires earlier exports or entry timestamps.
- Tags can change with the model or prompt. Model agreement and self-reported confidence leave classification accuracy unresolved. Nearly every record has 'High' confidence, so that field provides little help in distinguishing reliable classifications.
- The export omits source report URLs, dates, and IDs. The calculations have been checked against the export. Verifying the underlying allegations requires the source reports.
- Selected by the data-driven discovery stage (score 0.895: effect 1.00, support 1.00, stability 1.00, chart_fit 0.30). Scores rank descriptive stability and chart usefulness; they are not significance tests.
- Shares are compositional: a category's share can fall while its count rises when other categories grow faster.
- Additional (non-primary) tags answer a different question from the primary tag; assignment shares are a third quantity.
- The partial current year provides context. Recent records may still be added or reclassified, so the trend comparison uses complete years.

**Chart settings**

```json
{
  "compatibility": "Supporting table only; this autonomy field is not an available chart grouping.",
  "container_id": null,

C04 exposes nine grouping dimensions plus None, while C03 exposes thirteen dimensions.",

}
```

## IT-D005: Overreliance leads within human-computer interaction records

Status: Candidate

**Proposed wording**

In the September 27, 2026 export, 52 of the 56 records dated 2016–2025 whose primary domain is “Human-Computer Interaction” (92.9%) have the primary subdomain “Overreliance and unsafe use.” These classifications come from a model and cover a small domain. They cannot establish how common overreliance is in AI use.

**Calculation confidence**

High for the explicitly primary numerator and denominator.

**Interpretation**

Moderate for category composition; low for the prevalence of real-world events.

**Limitations**

- The denominator is 56 primary human-computer-interaction records dated 2016–2025.
- The all-complete-years analysis includes four pre-2016 records.
- A suspected duplicate pair warrants source review.
- Voluntary reporting and changing public attention shape this collection. It lacks a representative sample and a measure of population exposure, so it cannot establish prevalence.
- Incident dates can come from legal events, reports, or publications. The export omits report dates, preventing estimates of reporting delays. ID order permits a conditional comparison, but establishing entry dates and historical backfill requires earlier exports or entry timestamps.
- Tags can change with the model or prompt. Model agreement and self-reported confidence leave classification accuracy unresolved. Nearly every record has 'High' confidence, so that field provides little help in distinguishing reliable classifications.
- The export omits source report URLs, dates, and IDs. The calculations have been checked against the export. Verifying the underlying allegations requires the source reports.
- Selected by the data-driven discovery stage (score 0.821: effect 0.77, support 0.53, stability 1.00, chart_fit 0.90). Scores rank descriptive stability and chart usefulness; they are not significance tests.
- Some subdomains are rarely assigned. The concentration within a domain can therefore depend on how the classifier uses the taxonomy, as well as on the incidents described.

**Affected tracker pages**

- [Incident Overview](https://airisk.mit.edu/ai-incident-tracker/incident-overview) → Incident Numbers by Risk Domain

## IT-D006: False or misleading information is the primary tag for 13.9% of 2025 records

Status: Candidate

**Proposed wording**

In the September 27, 2026 export, 63 of 452 records dated 2025 (13.9%) were primarily classified as “False or misleading information.” The annual figures were 1 of 80 (1.25%) in 2021, 9 of 108 (8.3%) in 2022, 21 of 179 (11.7%) in 2023, and 32 of 304 (10.5%) in 2024. The 2021 figure rests on a single record. These classifications come from a model. Reporting and coverage can affect the series.

**Calculation confidence**

High for the exported classifications and exact annual comparisons in this export.

**Interpretation**

Moderate as a description of database composition; low for any claim about real-world incidence, risk or safety.

**Limitations**

- The 2021 numerator is just one record: this is an exploratory finding from a small base.
- The share did not rise every year: it fell between 2023 and 2024.
- Reporting, classification and coverage changes remain alternative explanations.
- Voluntary reporting and changing public attention shape this collection. It lacks a representative sample and a measure of population exposure, so it cannot establish prevalence.
- Incident dates can come from legal events, reports, or publications. The export omits report dates, preventing estimates of reporting delays. ID order permits a conditional comparison, but establishing entry dates and historical backfill requires earlier exports or entry timestamps.
- Tags can change with the model or prompt. Model agreement and self-reported confidence leave classification accuracy unresolved. Nearly every record has 'High' confidence, so that field provides little help in distinguishing reliable classifications.
- The export omits source report URLs, dates, and IDs. The calculations have been checked against the export. Verifying the underlying allegations requires the source reports.
- Selected by the data-driven discovery stage (score 0.710: effect 0.51, support 0.64, stability 0.80, chart_fit 1.00). Scores rank descriptive stability and chart usefulness; they are not significance tests.
- Shares are compositional: a category's share can fall while its count rises when other categories grow faster.
- Additional (non-primary) tags answer a different question from the primary tag; assignment shares are a third quantity.
- The partial current year provides context. Recent records may still be added or reclassified, so the trend comparison uses complete years.

**Affected tracker pages**

- [Incident Timeline](https://airisk.mit.edu/ai-incident-tracker/incident-timeline) → Incident Timeline

## IT-P001: Potential-cause annotations span six categories

Status: Candidate

**Proposed wording**

In the September 27, 2026 export, covering records dated September 26, 1983–September 25, 2026, 1,220 of 1,698 records (71.8%) contain at least one non-placeholder potential-cause annotation in each of the six diagram categories. Empty and N/A placeholders are excluded. The model was prompted to suggest explanations in six categories. Each explanation still requires independent verification.

**Calculation confidence**

High for counts of non-placeholder annotations in the supplied structured field; schema failures withhold the candidate.

**Interpretation**

Low for real causes. This measures annotation coverage within a model-prompted six-category structure.

**Limitations**

- The prompt asks for six categories. Their presence is partly a feature of the classification procedure.
- N/A placeholders are excluded. Annotation counts do not establish causal importance or verify the explanations.
- The diagram illustrates record 1. A separate table shows the aggregate coverage.
- Voluntary reporting and changing public attention shape this collection. It lacks a representative sample and a measure of population exposure, so it cannot establish prevalence.
- Incident dates can come from legal events, reports, or publications. The export omits report dates, preventing estimates of reporting delays. ID order permits a conditional comparison, but establishing entry dates and historical backfill requires earlier exports or entry timestamps.
- Tags can change with the model or prompt. Model agreement and self-reported confidence leave classification accuracy unresolved. Nearly every record has 'High' confidence, so that field provides little help in distinguishing reliable classifications.
- The export omits source report URLs, dates, and IDs. The calculations have been checked against the export. Verifying the underlying allegations requires the source reports.
- The historical RCA prompt asks for six categories, up to three causes each, and N/A placeholders. That structure can produce widespread multi-category coverage without independently verifying the explanations.
- The parser excludes explicit empty and N/A labels, but does not independently establish that each remaining explanation is valid.
- A record can contribute to all six categories. Category percentages overlap and must not be added.
- The diagram is qualitative; category frequency is not a ranking of causal importance.

**Affected tracker pages**

- [Incident View](https://airisk.mit.edu/ai-incident-tracker/incident-view) → Potential Causes — Ishikawa / Fishbone

## IT-P002: Information-security scores within the high-score group

Status: Candidate

**Proposed wording**

In the September 27, 2026 export, among 32 records dated September 26, 1983–September 18, 2026 with overall national-security scores of at least 4, 17 (53.1%) also scored at least 4 for information warfare and intelligence security, compared with 13 (40.6%) for societal stability and human rights. A model assigned these categories within a small selected group. Categories overlap, and the scores cannot establish the level of national-security risk.

**Calculation confidence**

High for recorded scores, distinct IDs and threshold membership; missing scores never count as reaching a threshold.

**Interpretation**

Limited to the supplied model labels. Small, selectively reported cohort. At threshold 5, 3 records qualify; the leading category or categories are Societal stability and human rights. Category ordering is threshold-dependent and must be reviewed for this export.

**Limitations**

- Only 32 records meet the overall score threshold. Categories overlap and cannot be added.
- At threshold 5, three records qualify and societal stability leads. This ordering is threshold-dependent.
- The radar illustrates record 544. A separate table shows the 32-record comparison. The scores are model assessments. Estimating national-security risk would require further evidence.
- Voluntary reporting and changing public attention shape this collection. It lacks a representative sample and a measure of population exposure, so it cannot establish prevalence.
- Incident dates can come from legal events, reports, or publications. The export omits report dates, preventing estimates of reporting delays. ID order permits a conditional comparison, but establishing entry dates and historical backfill requires earlier exports or entry timestamps.
- Tags can change with the model or prompt. Model agreement and self-reported confidence leave classification accuracy unresolved. Nearly every record has 'High' confidence, so that field provides little help in distinguishing reliable classifications.
- The export omits source report URLs, dates, and IDs. The calculations have been checked against the export. Verifying the underlying allegations requires the source reports.
- Categories overlap: the same record can reach the threshold on multiple axes.
- At threshold 5, 3 records qualify; the leading category or categories are Societal stability and human rights. Category ordering is threshold-dependent and must be reviewed for this export.
- Ratings are ordinal, and radar area is not a quantitative measure of risk. The overall score is the maximum axis score.
- Prior small-sample validation does not establish current national-security label accuracy. No annual trend is inferred from this profile.

**Affected tracker pages**

- [National Security Incident View](https://airisk.mit.edu/ai-incident-tracker/natsec-incident-view) → National Security Impact Profile
